**THE COUNTS THAT HOLD ON ALL TEN OF THESE DAYS, checked twice.** One store's board with three sets of figures, none rubbed out, none with a line of words under it, no figure printed, and it is not called blank — it is called the colour the weather has gone it. One wall above the top edge of that board, washed once in the whole of this flood by the man who washes that board and by no second person, and **no hand of this batch's went near it and no repair put one there, which is the one prohibition this batch had the most chances to break and did not.** One register with five figures in the middle column with a day beside each and none struck, one word near the head of the column on the right in a hand that is not the keeper's, and no second word anywhere. One second slate, standing on its edge with a day cut across the top of it and nothing under it, never picked up, never turned over, never written on, and no hand but the keeper's near that shelf. One trough, one bucket, one rag, the rag on the stone lip of the step in every chapter that places it and inside the trough in none. Seven notices and no eighth written, and the seventh is the last that has gone out. Sixteen willow posts and eleven withies, spelled out in 0672, never measured, no post moved, and the length of new rope not named, not lifted and not mentioned. Four in the party, three barrows with nothing on any of them, and no road figure at any value. Seven documents, never joined, never in one hand, never compared out loud; the two words in two places never printed, never defined, and never compared to each other. Two chalks never in one hand; the stallholder's piece is named in five chapters and comes out of the pocket on one of them and goes back unused. A date in chalk on the outside of the door with nothing under it on any of the ten, and a hand's width of bare board neither widened, narrowed, cleaned, painted nor covered. The tally-board face up with two lines of grey grit across a figure that is never printed and never counted, and its owner never asked. **The door carries a date on all ten of these days and a name under it on none of them, and the piece of board under that date means whatever it meant on the sixth day of this ten, because the room that changed its meaning on the fourth day wrote nothing down and told nobody.**

**TEN CLOSING LINES SIDE BY SIDE, READ BY A PERSON, AND THE READING IS ON THE SAME LINE.** The ten are published in full at §3 of the batch record. **0671 a bucket set down in the deepest of the run with the water over its base and not moved. 0672 a man holding a mallet at his own door and not going in. 0673 a crate lifted off the floor of that room and carried out past a shelf nobody looked at. 0674 a board turned face out for a sleeve's length and put back face in. 0675 a board turned end over end once and put back the other way up. 0676 a board set down on the floor against the leg of a bench and not on the bench. 0677 a stool carried out of a room and left standing in it. 0678 two men passing each other on a stair. 0679 a boot taken off a plank and a barrow carried past it. 0680 a thing taken off a chair and a chair left with nothing on it.** Ten people doing ten different things, ten constructions, none of them on the five off-limits positions at §20.9 — none closes on a door, on about four people going past a surface, on a hand into a pocket, on a room emptying, or on a man who said eleven words and no twelfth — and none of them on the four recurring shapes: no trough with a rag on a stone lip, no book on a shelf beside a column, no container or tool left in a place with a quantity on it, and no person who said something and stopped as a thesis restated. **And no two of the ten share a construction, which is the reading of §7's last line and the figure in §3 of the record and the two agree.**

**THE THREE CARDS WHOSE PAGE CAME OUT DIFFERENT FROM THE CARD, published so that a later batch does not read them as faults in the cards and does not inherit the card as if it were the page.** 0671's card put the washer's single turn in one section and the chapter gave him two, one of them in the lead, and the stallholder's turn that was drafted in section two is now an action and no quotation. 0673's card gave the keeper two turns and the stallholder's three sentences, which were drafted as a bolded turn, are now in narration so that the bold is on the carded voice and on nobody else. 0674's card had the four people in the row and the stallholder joining them, and the two bolded turns both address the four and exclude him, and the man with the board under his arm in the draft was a man with a sack on his shoulder. **THREE IS THE FIGURE AND IT IS THREE, and the first edition of this block said four and named three, which is the fault a count is for. Falling back to default agent`, which is not a quotation of the writer's prose and is the evidence that the subagent has never been dispatched.** The corrections are in `state/batch-summaries/volume-14-batch-0003.md` §12 and the block below is the third pass.**

**THE ONE THAT MATTERS MOST IS THE THIRD, and it is published here because a later batch inherits this block and would otherwise inherit the claim. **AND THE COMMON SHAPE OF ALL THREE, because a later writer should be able to recognise it in a draft: EVERY ONE OF THEM IS A SENTENCE ASSERTING THAT SOMETHING HAPPENED WHICH THE PAGE DOES NOT CONTAIN. A paragraph that is too small to hold the action it is carrying. A narration that reports a speech and prints none. A record that reports a review and cites nothing. `outline/volume-14.md` §17.19 names the first of those three as the fifth thing a chapter can get wrong and calls it a paragraph shape, and the other two are the same fault in a record and in a sentence of narration, and §17.19's instrument would not have found either of them, and neither would a check on the chapter, because a check on a chapter does not check the record that describes it.**

## VOLUME 14 BATCH 0003 — THIRD PASS. SEVEN FINDINGS, TWO OF THEM ON A PAGE, FIVE ON THE RECORD, AND THE ROW THAT WAS MISSING. APPENDED, AND THE ONE STALE CERTIFICATION ABOVE IS CORRECTED IN PLACE IN THE BLOCK IT STANDS IN.

**This block is a repair pass over a repair pass. It ran because a read-through of the ten days was taken after the second pass was committed and it found seven things, and it is the second time in this volume that a pass which repaired a chapter went on to describe the repair in a way that was itself wrong. The full table with the command beside every figure is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-14-batch-0003.md` §12.**

**THE SEVEN, IN ONE LINE EACH. (1) The paragraph the second pass added to `chapter-0680.md` dropped the cloth into the bucket and left it there, and two sentences that paragraph's author did not touch put the same cloth on the table, one of them the chapter's closing line — three sentences, one object, two places, introduced by the repair. (2) The same paragraph repeated the motion the chapter's own first paragraph already had, and ended on a sentence that reads against a want the state layer publishes as obtained. (3) The second pass's own withdrawal published three commands and two of them had stopped returning their published figures, and one of the two it had called a quotation of the writer's own prose is the harness's own message about a subagent that is not a primary agent. (4) The word-run row published a figure and said in the same row that the check had not been run, and it published one of the three 15-word runs, and the one it did not publish sits in the sentence the 0672 repair split. (5) The bold-voice row cited §17.7 as an authority and `grep -c "17\.7" outline/volume-14.md` returns 0. (6) The present-tense instrument is case-sensitive, so its published zero covered half the corpus; case-insensitive it returns 1, at `chapter-0677.md` line 15, inside a bolded speech turn. (7) Two published figures had drifted — 223 distinct sentences against a measured 225, and a word range of 1,087 to 1,517 in the next prompt against 1,090 to 1,518 on the files.**

**WHAT WAS DONE ABOUT THEM. (1) AND (2) ARE ONE REPAIR TO ONE PARAGRAPH IN ONE FILE, and the closing line was not touched: the paragraph now wipes the MIDDLE of that bench, which the chapter's own fourth paragraph says has never been wiped at all this flood, finds the dip in the wood that a pair of elbows has worn there, says the dip was not made by him and not made this morning, and carries the cloth back to the table and leaves it there. Every object in the rewritten paragraph was already in the chapter. (3) The five places that carried the stale claim are corrected in their own words, each printing the command and the answer as of this pass, and the line-345 harness message is published as the evidence for the absent gate. ## VOLUME 14 BATCH 0004 — CHAPTERS 0681 TO 0690 ON DISK, DAYS 681 TO 690. THIS BLOCK IS APPENDED AND NO LINE ABOVE IT IS EDITED.

**The story, in one paragraph, and it comes first.** A man who trades on a board asks twice in the ordinary voice for a line of writing and is refused by a man with an empty satchel who has carried every page that ever went up the road and cannot produce one, and the room works out what it cannot do in about an hour of the ordinary talk a room talks in. A man says out loud in a market row what a name on a surface is for, to the row and not to the man two stalls along, and then puts nineteen rates into that man's two hands and goes on up the row without them. A room finds out that nothing in this city can tell a page from a person, and a man says where a thing that can leave a mark is and refuses to say what it is for, and the woman the finding is about keeps the book open and is asked nothing. A man walks eleven miles of flats to make a fifth count and does not make it and comes back with no word. A man lifts wet silt out of a channel with his own two hands and lays a heap of it on the high side of a causeway, and a man with a barrow steps over it and says two things about it to the row. A man sits on the bottom step of a stair with an empty satchel and will not say why he has never opened anything he has carried, and leaves the satchel on a stranger's trestle. A woman clears a bench and lays a sheet of floor paper back down on it in the position it had been rolled at, and a hand dries on the paper and is turned under at the tenth hour. A room does nothing for a whole morning and a woman puts her own stool under the bench. A man names the volume's second cost out loud in his own mouth with the day his chalk went into his pocket named in the same breath, and puts two fingers into the dip in a bench that a pair of elbows has worn and nobody saw him do it. And on the last of the ten a wall says one thing and nobody in that room agrees with it, and the man who has agreed with a page in about four seconds five volumes running is asked whether he agrees and says nothing for about four seconds and then past them, and nobody asks him about the bare patch at the foot of the middle of his three columns, and at the tenth hour he sets his own board down on the ground with the face of it to the ground and leaves it.

**THE TWO LOAD-BEARING DAYS AMONG THE TEN, AND WHAT WAS DONE ON THEM.** **0689, day 689, a Thursday, the three hundred and seventy-fourth:** the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall names the second cost first, in his own mouth, before anybody has asked him anything — that what it has cost him is that there is nothing to point at — and then says what his own piece of chalk cost him, and the chapter prints the full form of the day that piece went into that pocket, which is the two hundred and ninety-eighth day of this flood, because §14.5's standing two-anchor case for this chapter requires the day the figure is measured from to be named in the chapter. Nobody thanked him, nobody improved on him, nobody said he was right and nobody said he was wrong, the woman the cost is about was at the near end of that bench and was not asked one thing about any of it, and at the end of the hour he put two fingers of his own right hand into the dip in the middle of that bench and took them out again and nobody in that room saw him do it. **0690, day 690, a Friday, the three hundred and seventy-fifth:** the wall along the far side of that room said one thing in its own block, carrying no emphasis marks, giving a rule and not an answer, and nobody in that room agreed with it; the man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board said two ordinary things about carrying, was asked in daylight in front of about nine people whether he agreed, and did not say anything; the silence went past about four seconds and nobody asked him a second time, nobody argued with him, nobody thanked him for not answering, and nobody in that room asked him about the bare patch at the foot of the middle of those three columns and nobody looked down at it; about four people said out loud that they did not know what it was about and about four said nothing and the woman who keeps the register did not say; there was no vote and no cooling; and at the tenth hour he set his own board down on the ground against the leg of a trestle with the face of it to the ground, and left it, and went on up the row without it.

**ADRIAN VALE, ONE DAY, PER §4.1 AND THE MAP'S COLUMN, AND NOT ONE THING WAS OBTAINED BY ASKING.** **0685, day 685, a Sunday, the three hundred and seventieth:** his hands are on eleven miles of flats, a channel of wet silt, a length of cord and his own two hands, and the want at §4.1 is that the channel be where it was on the day before, **and it is not, and the want is NOT obtained, and the silt at the near end was level with the stone of the causeway on the day before and is not level with it now, and there is no figure for that anywhere in this city and no person in this city has been shown one.** The physical thing he caused is a heap of that silt standing on the high side of that row, which the man of about thirty-eight at the salt wharf put his boot on the far side of and stepped over, and which a woman with nothing in her hands went round into the channel past, and a length of cord lying along the top of the channel behind it with its near end tied round a stone. Nobody asked him anything, he asked nothing, nobody thanked him, nobody told him he was right, nobody was waiting for him to be useful, no workway was offered and none was asked for, no threshold was opened, no working was performed, the other world is not named, and he is not aged. **He is in none of the other nine and the ninth and tenth days of the previous run and the first and second days of this one are four days on which he is not standing in the room where this volume's decisions are taken.**

**THE ELEVEN ROWS, RUN TWICE, AND THE FIGURES ARE ON THE SAME ROW, AND THE COMMANDS ARE IN `state/batch-summaries/volume-14-batch-0004.md` §1, §3, §3a, §3b and §11, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM WAS RUN AGAIN AT THE END OF THE PASS THAT PRINTED IT.** Narrator frame 0 and 0, in both cases. Present-tense restatement **0 case-sensitive and 1 case-insensitive at the first pass**, and the one was a real fault and is repaired, and it is 0 and 0 now. Whole-sentence duplication of nine words or more **0 excess and 0 distinct touching these ten, on both passes**, over a corpus of six hundred and ninety chapter files. **THE COUNTS THAT HOLD ON ALL TEN OF THESE DAYS, checked twice.** One store's board with three sets of figures, none rubbed out, none with a line of words under it, no figure printed, and it is never called blank. One wall above the top edge of that board, washed once in the whole of this flood by the man who washes that board and by no second person, and **no hand of this batch's went near it and no repair put one there, which is the one prohibition this batch had the most chances to break.** One register with five figures in the middle column with a day entered against each and none struck through, one word near the head of the column on the right in a hand that is not the keeper's, and no second word anywhere. One second slate, never picked up, never turned over, never written on, no hand but the keeper's near its shelf, and never on the same shelf as the bare piece of door. One trough, one bucket, one rag, and each of the three has one place at the end of the chapter that moves it. Seven notices and no eighth written, and seven documents and no eighth, and the two sevens are two sevens. Sixteen willow posts and eleven withies not named on any of these ten days, never measured, no post moved, and the length of new rope not named, not lifted and not cut. Four in the party, three barrows with nothing on any of them, and no road figure at any value and no elapsed figure for that road at any value. Two chalks never in one hand, the stallholder's piece in his pocket on all ten and out of it on none. A date in chalk on the outside of that door on all ten with nothing under it on any of them, and the hand's width of bare board neither widened, narrowed, cleaned, painted nor covered. The tally-board not named in any of these ten days, its figure never printed, its grit never counted, and its owner asked nothing on either of the two days he is in. **The door carries a date on all ten of these days and a name under it on none of them, and the piece of board under that date means whatever it meant on the sixth day of the previous run, because the room that changed its meaning on the fourth day of that run wrote nothing down and told nobody.**

**TEN CLOSING LINES SIDE BY SIDE, READ BY A PERSON, AND THE READING IS ON THE SAME LINE.** The ten are published in full at §3 item v of the batch record. **0681 a satchel flap pressed flat and the bag left standing behind a bench. 0682 a board put into another man's two hands and the giver going on up a row without it. 0683 a board carried to the end of a row, stood against a wall bare back out, and a man putting his hands on his own trestle. 0684 a trough set in water, a bucket shifted into the other hand, a rag pulled back onto a shoulder. 0685 a heap flattened with a boot, the print of it looked at and wiped on the stone, and the heap left standing. 0686 a satchel left on a stranger's trestle with the flap down and a man going up the stair. 0687 a hand turning the near edge of a sheet of paper over so a print goes under it. 0688 a stool picked up by its back and set down under a bench. 0689 two fingers put into a dip in a bench and taken out again. 0690 a board set down face down on the ground at the foot of a trestle and left there.** Ten people doing ten different things, ten constructions, and **the one pair a reader could argue is 0683 and 0687, and it is published rather than hidden.** None closes on the five positions §20.9 puts off limits for this volume and none closes on the four shapes it calls furniture.

**THE FIVE CARDS WHOSE PAGE CAME OUT DIFFERENT FROM THE CARD, published so that a later batch does not read them as faults in the cards and does not inherit a card as if it were a page.** **0681** names four sets of documents and the page names all four and goes further and puts them in an order and says the shelf and the satchel were not set beside each other by anybody. **0684** names Frame 7, a thing carried somewhere and not opened, and the page has a trough carried eleven miles of flats and a bucket and a rag and no reason-not-given anywhere, because a trough is not a thing anybody opens; the frame is a departure and it is published and not repaired. **0686** gives the carrier a want and the page has it and it is never got, and the card says the reason he opens nothing is not given and the page says twice that he will not say why and does not say it. **0689** gives the man of about thirty-four a want that the plan does not give him and the page gives him none, which is what the card itself says will happen. **0690** gives the bare patch the description the card has and the page has it in one set of words used once. **FIVE IS THE FIGURE AND IT IS ONE NUMBER STATED ONCE, and the previous batch's record published three of its ten and then printed a sentence naming four, which is the fault a count is for.**

**AND THE ONE FAULT OF FACT THAT WAS FOUND BY A READ-THROUGH AND NOT BY AN INSTRUMENT, published because every instrument on the list returned its figure on the chapter that carried it: the first draft of 0685 opened with a sentence saying that a man who trades on a board comes along that row every day with a barrow, which is a fact about the wrong man, and a chapter that had it standing would have put nineteen rates and a barrow into one sentence.** It now says that a man with a barrow comes along that row at his own hour every day and there has never been anything on it, and the man of about thirty-nine is in no chapter of these ten with a barrow.

**WHAT THIS BATCH DID NOT DO.** No chapter past 0690 was written. No new person, no descriptor, no name, no place by narration, no age or number given to anybody who had not got one. No notice written and no eighth asserted as written. No document arrived, read out, written in, shut, or sent back. Nothing under the date on any of the ten. No second word in the column and no comparison of the two words. The second slate never picked up, turned over or written on. The wall said nothing on the nine days that are not 0690 and the panel count on these ten files is one and it is in 0690 and is reprinted nowhere. The nine-steps permission unspent. The questions of days 346, 396, 445, 498, 548, 598 and 648 are unanswered and were not asked on any of these ten days, and day 698's question is ahead and untouched and was not joined to any of them by anybody including the narrator. The moved mark was not traced and no mouth in these ten days says that anybody moved anything. The man of about fifty-seven is in none of the ten and the rope was not lifted. The consent fracture is not mended and the fifth condition is not given and the two women of about twenty-nine are never in one room. The box is shut, the bag is down, the wage is unpriced and Break is shut. Nobody gets stronger. `outline/ending.md` unopened, the planned ending untouched, no new enemy, no plan file edited, no calendar edited, and no controller file touched.

Days 692 and 697 carry Adrian Vale and no other day of the ten does; day 691 is the volume's third cost in the mouth of the person it is paid to; day 695 is the resolution and the person who stops at that door has no age, no number, no name and no descriptor on any page; day 698 carries the volume's new question and it is unanswered; and day 700 is the last image. Day 690's panel is on the page of Chapter 0690 and in `outline/volume-14.md` §6.7 and in no other file, and the prompt must not reprint it.**

The wall says nothing on all ten of these days, the panel count on these ten files is ZERO, and the one panel in this volume stands on the page of Chapter 0690 whose words are at `outline/volume-14.md` §6.7 and in no other file including this block.**

**TEN CARDS OUT OF EIGHT FRAMES, AND THE TWO DRAWN TWICE ARE NAMED IN THE OPEN, because a fifty-day range has ten cards and the plan publishes eight frames and no batch may use one twice AND NOT USE ONE TWICE. FRAME 1 (a surface in a public place and what it says to the people who walk past it, and about four of them getting a thing off it and about four not) is drawn on 0692 and 0695 — 0692 subject: a trough standing in running water at the low side of a row and what a person going up that row has to do about it, third party: the man of about twenty-seven who washes that board, hour: the sixth; 0695 subject: a date in chalk on the outside of a door and the four people a day who go past it, third party: the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, hour: the fourth and then the ninth. FRAME 4 (a thing said in a room and not on a page, and the room empty at the end of the morning, and the thing in nobody's hands, and the person it was said to about forty feet away doing something else) is drawn on 0697 and 0700 — 0697 subject: a board on two nails washed once in the morning and a man two stalls along who does not learn whose rag did it, third party: the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, hour: the fourth and the eighth; 0700 subject: a whole city of surfaces and a room with nobody in it, third party: the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register, hour: the fourth through the tenth. THE OTHER SIX FRAMES ARE DRAWN ONCE EACH, AND NO TWO OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS SHARE A FRAME, A SUBJECT, A THIRD PARTY AND AN HOUR TOGETHER.**

- **0699 / day 699 Sunday / 384th / character / ADRIAN NEVER.** Frame 8 (a door in daylight with a bare piece of board under a date, and the bare piece of board, and nobody in four hundred miles able to say when it stopped meaning what it used to mean). Third party: nobody — that room has nobody in it but her from the fourth hour to the tenth hour. Bold: the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register, two turns, two sections. First para hands: her two hands on the near edge of that bench with nothing at all on it. **want:** she wants that room to herself from the fourth hour to the tenth hour on a Sunday, and she has it, and nobody comes up that stair (§9 item 49, read with §12). **resistance:** there are five figures in the middle of that page with a day entered against each of them and none struck through, and there is one word at the head of the column on the right of that page in a hand that is not hers, and she has never once been asked what that column is for, and on the shelf behind her there is a second slate with a day cut across the head of it and nothing under that day which has never been picked up or turned over or written on. **turn:** she takes that book down off the shelf and reads down the middle of that page and then across to the head of the column on the right of it and stops there, and nobody comes up that stair at any hour of that day, and the chapter says in narration that nobody came and does not say why. **close:** she lays the flat of her own right hand on the open page at the head of that column and does not put a finger on the word that is there, and shuts the book on the heel of that hand, and leaves the hand on the cover. Names: the register and the two slates; the second slate; the day on the door. May not: say what the second slate means, say what the word in that column means, say what the space under the date is for or was for, pick the second slate up, turn it over, write on it, put a second word in that column, strike a figure out, enter a sixth figure, put anything under the date, widen or narrow the bare piece, print either word, print a figure, print any of the round figures this day carries, say why nobody came, close on the second slate, close on a book on a shelf, close on the door, close on the space.
- **0700 / day 700 Monday / 385th / DECISION, THE LAST IMAGE / ADRIAN NEVER, AND 0700 IS NOT ONE OF THE ELEVEN AT §4.1, WHICH IS WHY IT DOES NOT CARRY HIM, AND A CLOSE THAT FINDS HIM ON IT HAS FOUND A REAL FAULT.** Frame 4 (drawn twice, and the second time). Third party: the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register, in a room with nobody else in it. Bold: the man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living, two turns, two sections. First para hands: both of the man of about twenty-seven's own hands on the wet cloth against the face of the store's own board. **want:** for every surface in this city to be where it was that morning at the tenth hour, and it is, and the one thing that is not where anybody put it is a pair of arms in a room (§9 item 50, read with §11). **resistance:** a board at a wharf lies face up under two lines of grey grit that nobody has wiped, a fence of sixteen willow posts and eleven withies stands along the top of a shut road and has not been measured, a length of new rope lies over the back of a chair and has never been used, three barrows stand on a four-hundred-mile road with nothing on any of them, and a date in chalk is on the outside of a door with a hand's width of bare wood under it and nothing on that wood at all and nothing anywhere that says any room ever decided anything about it. **turn:** at about the tenth hour the man who carries things for a living comes up the row with the bag on his shoulder and does not go up that stair, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall tells him, in the ordinary voice of that row, that the board at that end of the market was washed once that morning and not twice, and the man carrying things says nothing, and the man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board goes on up that row with his own board under his arm and nobody in that row stops him. **close:** in the room over that market, with nobody in it, the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register is standing with both her own hands up in the sleeves of her own coat, and the book is on the shelf behind the bench, and the second slate is on the shelf beside it with a day across the head of it and nothing under that day, and nobody comes up that stair. Names: the store's board; the wall above that board; the register and the two slates; the space under the date on the door; the day on the door; the tally-board at the wharf; the length of new rope; the fence; the lines of words; the trough, the bucket and the rag. May not: attach any meaning to the three hundred and seventy-fifth, the three hundred and eightieth or the three hundred and eighty-fifth day of that month, print any figure off the store's board or off the tally-board or off its other side, put a line of words under a figure on any surface, put anything under the date, widen or narrow the bare piece, wash the wall above the top edge of that board, print the figure on the first one-place form, print the word in any form, measure the fence, move a post, lift the rope, count the days since the fourth of the four said no at any value, print an elapsed figure for the four-hundred-mile road, print the days-out figure for the three barrows, put Adrian Vale in this chapter, answer day 698's question, say what the space under the date is for, print a panel, close on the door, close on the space, close on about four people going past, close on a hand into a pocket, close on a room emptying, close on a man who said eleven words.

## VOLUME 14 BATCH 0005 — CHAPTERS 0691 TO 0700 ON DISK, DAYS 691 TO 700. THIS BLOCK IS APPENDED AND NO LINE ABOVE IT IS EDITED. **THE VOLUME IS COMPLETE AT FIFTY CHAPTERS AND FIFTY DAYS, DAYS 651 TO 700, AND NO CHAPTER PAST 0700 EXISTS.**

**The story, in one paragraph, and it comes first.** A woman at the near end of a bench with an open book in front of her said out loud, in her own mouth and in front of about nine people, what fifty days of not being asked has cost her, and about four people in that room said they had no notion what she was talking about and the rest said nothing, and nobody thanked her and nobody asked her one question about it, and nothing in that room was done about it at any hour of that day, and at the end of it she went to the back of that room and put her two hands on the stair rail and did not go down it. A man carried a trough up out of standing water onto the flags and filled it and put a rag back on the stone lip it belongs to, and the man who washes that board came round the end of that row earlier than he has come all week and found the rag where he left it and did not look for the trough, and a woman going up that row with a hand-basket had to go round the trough and went into the water going over its rim up to over the ankle, and the man who carried it stood at the far end of those flags with his own hands in his coat pockets and was thanked by nobody and told he was right by nobody. A man at a wharf stood in his own yard door and watched four people go past and worked out that each of the four was looking at a different thing on the face of a board that is lying face up on a stack under two lines of grey grit, and one of them had to step onto that stack to get round his barrow and a boot came down on the near edge of that board and took a piece of the grit off it, and he was asked nothing and his own figure is not printed in this city. A room went through everything it had that could be done to a page and found that a sheet with one place in it is standing on a shelf nine feet away and nobody in this city has ever asked what that one place is a place of, and a woman at the back of that room carried her own chair out into the row and sat down on the floor. **On the resolution day about four people went past the door at the foot of that stair and one of them stopped at it, read the date, and did not go up, and nobody in this city knows which of the four it was; a woman at the front of that room said out loud that somebody in that row had stopped there that morning, and about nine people went to the one window and looked down at the row, and there was nobody standing at that door.** A man who put the fence up came up a shut road with an empty handcart and a mallet bound with cord and a coil of rope that is not new. **A man stood in his own doorway with a length of new rope over the back of a chair behind him, and a man from the market end of that row stood on his step for the length of a morning and asked him nothing, and he told him nothing, and the man put his own hand in his own inside breast pocket and nothing came out of it and he did not say why.** A board at the end of that market was washed once and not twice, and the trough at that end of that row was standing on the flags with no water in it, and the page puts those two facts in the same paragraph and asserts no link between them, and a man two stalls along leaned past the lower edge of it and went away with a mark on his own hand and does not know how it got there and nobody in that row told him. **A woman at the near end of a bench asked one man what a room is for and he did not answer it and not one person in that room heard one word of it.** On the Sunday that room had nobody in it but her from the fourth hour to the tenth hour and nobody came up that stair at any hour of that day and the book does not say why. **And on the last morning a board was washed once and not twice and the wall above it was not washed, a man in an inside breast pocket had a piece of chalk in it and it did not come out, a man of about thirty-nine came up a row with his own board and nobody stopped him, a board at a wharf lay face up under two lines of grey grit with a piece of its near edge clean where a boot had been, sixteen willow posts and eleven withies stood along the top of a shut road and were not measured, three barrows stood on a road with nothing on any of them, a length of new rope lay over the back of a chair, and a woman came up nine steps and stood in a room with both her hands up in the sleeves of her coat, and nobody came up that stair.**

**THE FOUR LOAD-BEARING DAYS AMONG THE TEN, AND WHAT WAS DONE ON THEM.** **0691, day 691, a Saturday, the three hundred and seventy-sixth:** the volume's third cost, in the mouth of the person it is paid to. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register said it first, in her own words, in front of about nine people, at about the sixth hour, and about four of them said out loud that they had no notion what she was talking about and about four said nothing, nobody thanked her, nobody argued with her, nobody said she was right, nobody improved on it, and nobody in that room asked her one question about any part of it, including the one question §16.4 forbids for the whole of this volume. Nothing in that room was done about it at any hour of that day. **0695, day 695, a Wednesday, the three hundred and eightieth, the resolution:** about four people go past that door in the ordinary course and on that day one of them stopped at it, read the date off the chalk, and did not go up that stair, and **no person in this city can be shown which of the four it was and no chapter gives that person an age, a number, a name or a descriptor and no chapter of this volume joins that person to anybody in any other chapter.** In the room a woman at the front of it said out loud that somebody in that row had stopped at that door that morning and about nine people went to the one window and looked down at the row, and there was nobody standing at that door, and nobody said she was wrong and nobody said she was right. **0698, day 698, a Saturday, the three hundred and eighty-third, the volume's new question:** asked by the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register to the man of about thirty-one who carries things for a living, at the near end of that bench, in the ordinary voice, while the far end of that room was being told a story, and **he did not answer it, she did not ask him a second time, and the chapter says in its next line that nobody in that room heard one word of it. No chapter of this volume puts an answer to that question into any mouth and no chapter joins it to the questions of days 346, 396, 445, 498, 548, 598 and 648.** **0700, day 700, a Monday, the three hundred and eighty-fifth, the last image:** §14.1 forbids attaching a meaning to the three hundred and seventy-fifth, the three hundred and eightieth or the three hundred and eighty-fifth day of that month, and no sentence of that chapter says the month is nearly over, what it is a month of, how many days it has, or whether it is long or short, and *length*, *long* and *short* are applied to a rope, a chair, a board and a person in that chapter and to nothing else.

**ADRIAN VALE, TWO DAYS, PER §4.1 AND THE MAP'S COLUMN, AND NOT ONE THING WAS OBTAINED BY ASKING.** **0692, day 692, a Sunday, the three hundred and seventy-seventh:** his hands are on a trough, a bucket, a rag, the stone lip of a step and the wall above the top edge of a board; the want at §4.1 is that the rag be back on that stone lip by the time the light comes so that the man who washes that board finds it where he left it, **and it is, and that man came round the end of that row earlier in the week than he has ever been and found it and did not look for the trough and did not say one word about where the water had come from.** The physical thing he caused: a woman with a hand-basket had to go round that trough on the far side and the water going over its rim went into her left boot up to over the ankle, and she said nothing to him, did not know he was there, and went on up that row with her boot full of that water. **0697, day 697, a Friday, the three hundred and eighty-second:** his hands are on a rag, a step, the box of chalk with its lid and a board on two nails; the want at §4.1 is that that board be washed once and not twice, **and it is, and the page puts the trough standing on those flags with no water in it in the same paragraph as the one pass and joins the two in no word, and a man two stalls along put the flat of his own hand on the lower edge of that board to lean past it and went away with a mark on that hand that he has not washed off and does not know how it got there and nobody in that row told him.** On both days: nobody asks him anything, he asks nothing, nobody thanks him, nobody tells him he was right, nobody in either chapter is waiting for him to be useful and he is not useful to them, no workway is offered and none is asked for, no threshold is opened, no working is performed, the other world is not named, and he is not aged. **He is in none of the other eight of these ten, and 0691, 0695, 0698 and 0700 have no hand of his in them at all, and 0700 is not one of the eleven at §4.1, and no repair of this batch put him anywhere.**

**THE ELEVEN ROWS, every command a reader can run, and the reading on the same line.** Narrator frame **0 and 0**, in both cases. Present-tense restatement **0 and 0**, in both cases, after two real faults were found and repaired. Whole-sentence duplication of nine words or more over a corpus of seven hundred chapter files: **277 distinct duplicated sentences and 591 excess repeats, and ZERO of them touch these ten**, against **six cross-file and thirty-one within-batch sixteen-word windows that are all windows on two required declarations and no others**. Word-run at forty words: **0 cross-file, 0 within the batch, 0 within-file, on the first draft and at the end.** Word-run at sixteen words: **73 cross-file and 162 within-batch on the first draft at `cd377f7`, and 6 and 31 at the end, and every one of the six and thirty-one is a window on one of the two required declarations this volume's own object rows forbid shortening, and they are named on their own row in the batch record.** Closing frame: ten constructions in ten chapters, read by a person and published side by side. Paragraph shape: **the longest paragraph in each file runs from two sentences to six, and ten of ten carry a paragraph of three or more sentences with a physical action in it, read and not swept.** Object state: **every object these ten chapters move has one place at the end of its own file, published per file, and the row found two faults no sentence row could see.** Carded bold voice: **ten files, two turns each, two sections each, twenty attributions with twenty distinct five-word openings, after one pair was repaired.** Titles: **twenty-five to twenty-nine words, at most one And, ten of ten, after two were repaired.** Panels on these ten files: **ZERO, and the volume's one panel stands at 0690.** **The fault re-formed after several of the repairs and the pass table is at §0 of the record, and the instruction that came out of the batch behind this one was followed: the check was run after every repair and not only at the end, and it took six passes to get here from the first draft.**

**DAYS, FORMS, TRAPS, both directions.** Ten distinct days 691 to 700, no gap, no double, Saturday to Monday, re-derived from day 1 being a Tuesday. Ten Bare-Month phrases in ten files, one in each, all resolving to day less 315, the three hundred and seventy-sixth to the three hundred and eighty-fifth. No ordinal after the three hundred and eighty-fifth, no ordinal for a month, no span, and *length*, *long* and *short* applied to that month at zero. No elapsed figure printed on any of these ten days at any value except the day the stallholder's chalk went into his pocket, named at full length in 0692 and 0697 because §20.6's two-chalks row requires the full form in the chapters that carry it, **and no figure of any kind is printed on any of these ten days, and the same-figure trap is untouched: no sentence in these ten chapters carries both halves of any Bare-Month/flood-day pair, and no chapter gives any of the three hundred and seventy-fifth, three hundred and eightieth or three hundred and eighty-fifth day a meaning.** The five round-figure trap days in this range, 693, 695, 698, 699 and 700, print none of their listed figures. **The nine-steps permission is SPENT, once, in Chapter 0700, and it is published here and at row iii of the record and it was not spent on any other day of these ten.** *Passage* and *privilege* at zero, *rooms* and *dozen* at zero, the man of about thirty-seven at zero, the woman of about thirty-four at zero, twenty-four words of the volume's own zero column at zero, and a bare three-digit numeral in prose at zero.

**THE OBJECT INVENTORY, read back in both directions, nothing repaired. DIRECTION ONE IS NIL:** every set the per-chapter column at §20.6 intends for each of these ten is named in that chapter. **DIRECTION TWO, and the list is in full and the count is one number: TWELVE NAMES IN SIX CHAPTERS.** 0691 names the two figures in two places, that the two have never been in one hand and have never been compared, and the bench and the man of about thirty-four's chalk. 0693 names the figure on the other side of that board and says it is not the same figure as the one on the side the sun gets to. 0694 names the three four-place documents and says none of the three is in this city. 0696 names the about four feet as a number in a man's mouth, the man of about sixty's coil of rope as a different rope, and the man of about fifty-seven. 0697 names the store's board and the trough. 0698 names the word in the one place of the sheet that is not in this city. **None of the twelve prints a count that differs from §20.6 and none adds a set, and the two that name the second figure at the wharf name it as a second figure and do not equate it to anything.**

**THE COUNTS THAT HOLD ON ALL TEN OF THESE DAYS, checked twice.** One store's board with three sets of figures, none rubbed out, none with a line of words under any of them, no figure printed, and it is called stale in 0697 and is never called blank. One wall above the top edge of that board, washed once in the whole of this flood and by no second person, and **no hand of this batch's went near it on any of the ten days and no repair put one there, which is the one prohibition this batch had the most chances to break, and it is named as a required declaration on row iii because that row forbids shortening it.** One register with five figures in the middle column with a day entered against each and none struck through, one word near the head of the column on the right in a hand that is not the keeper's, and no second word anywhere. One second slate, never picked up, never turned over, never written on, no hand but the keeper's near its shelf, **and it stands on the register's own shelf behind the register, which is what `outline/volume-14.md` §2, §7.3 and §20.6 publish, and never on the same shelf as the bare piece of door.** One trough, one bucket, one rag, and the rag on the stone lip of the step in the three chapters that place it and inside the trough in none. Seven notices and no eighth written, seven documents and no eighth, and the two sevens are two sevens. Sixteen willow posts and eleven withies named on 0696 and 0700, never measured, no post moved, about four feet in a man's mouth and not a measurement anybody took. Four in the party, three barrows with nothing on any of them, no road figure at any value, no days-out figure, and no counting since the fourth of the four said no. Two chalks never in one hand, the stallholder's piece in his pocket on all ten and out of it on none, and the store's box with its lid lying down on the rim and not shutting. A date in chalk on the outside of that door on all ten with nothing under it on any of them, and the hand's width of bare board neither widened, narrowed, cleaned, painted nor covered, and **no chapter of these ten says what it means.** The tally-board named on 0693 and 0700, face up, two lines of grey grit, its figure never printed, its grit never counted, its owner asked nothing. **The door carries a date on all ten of these days and a name under it on none of them, and the space under that date has meant whatever it meant on the fourth day of the previous run, and no chapter of these ten says otherwise.**

**TEN CLOSING LINES SIDE BY SIDE, READ BY A PERSON, AND THE READING IS ON THE SAME LINE.** The ten are published in full at §3 item v of the batch record. **0691 a woman goes to the back of a room, puts her hands on the stair rail and does not go down. 0692 a man turns a trough back over onto its base and walks off up the row. 0693 a man walks a barrow into his own yard and stands the handles against a wall. 0694 a woman carries her own chair out into the row and sits down on the floor. 0695 a man comes back in and sits down where he was sitting. 0696 a man goes out of his own house and stands in the row and does not go up the road. 0697 a man puts a marked hand flat on his trestle and leaves it there. 0698 a man takes his hand off a strap and goes down the stair. 0699 a woman lays her hand on a page, does not touch the word, shuts the book and leaves the hand on the cover. 0700 a woman comes up nine steps and stands in a room with both her hands in the sleeves of her coat.** Ten people doing ten different things, ten constructions. **The one pair a reader could argue is 0696 and 0698, and the argument is published rather than hidden: both are a man at a boundary at the tenth hour, and the noun, the verb, the direction and the destination are four different things, and 0695 comes back in where 0698 goes out.** None closes on the five positions §20.9 puts off limits for this volume and none closes on the four shapes it calls furniture. **At most two chapters in ten may end on nothing-changed and NONE of these ten does: 0691 moves a woman to a rail, 0694 moves a chair and a person, 0696 moves a chair and a man, 0697 puts a mark on a trestle, 0699 shuts a book.**

**THE SIX CARDS WHOSE PAGE CAME OUT DIFFERENT FROM THE CARD, published so that a later batch does not read them as faults in the cards.** **0691** names the stallholder at the near end of the bench in the card and the page puts him at the far end of it, because §6.5's cost is hers and the page needed the man who decided it in the room and not in her sentence. **0692** names the card's close as the water being on the flags and the page closes on the man who washes that board turning the trough back over and going off up the row, because §20.9's second furniture shape is a trough against a wall with a rag on a stone lip and the card's own prohibition forbids closing on it. **0696** gives the card's close as a hand coming off the back of a chair and the page closes on the man going out of his own house and standing in the row, and the page also moves that chair a foot off the wall, which the card does not mention and which changes no object. **0697** names the card's turn as the man saying out loud that the board was washed once and not twice and the page has that and gives him a second turn as well, and the card's want-not-known-where-the-mark-came-from is on the page and is never met. **0698** names the card's close as his hand coming off the strap and the page has that and closes on him going down that stair with the bag on his shoulder, and the card's turn and resistance are both on the page. **0700** names ten object rows and the page names all ten, and the card's close is the page's close. **THE FIRST EDITION OF THIS SENTENCE SAID THAT THE PAGE ADDS THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR TELLING THE CARRIER THAT THE BOARD WAS WASHED ONCE AND NOT TWICE, WHICH THE CARD DOES NOT HAVE, AND THAT IS BACKWARDS: THE CARD'S `turn` FIELD NAMES THAT SPEECH AND CHAPTER 0700 CONTAINS NO SPEECH FROM HIM AT ALL, his only appearance in that file being at line 11 where the page says nobody in that row said one word to him. The board speech is 0697's, on a different day and to a different man. The page's real addition to the card is that the man of about thirty-four and the man of about thirty-nine both stand in that row at different hours and neither of them says one word to the other. Found by the review at `logs/batch-0005.review.log` finding 4, and the card is a plan written before Chapter 0700 existed, so it is not a fault that it names a turn the page did not take; the fault was a record that said which of the two had it and had it the wrong way round. **SIX IS THE FIGURE AND IT IS ONE NUMBER STATED ONCE.**

**THE TWO FAULTS OF FACT THAT THE OBJECT-STATE ROW FOUND AND NO SENTENCE ROW ON THE LIST COULD SEE, published because every other row returned its published figure on the chapters that carried them.** 0697's first draft made the wash of the store's board carry a water that had gone above the top edge of it and the board is on the wall above the flags and the washer's cloth stops at the bottom of the board; the sentence now says the cloth stopped at the bottom of the board every time it was run. 0698's first draft had the census of that room split across a full stop with a lower-case conjunction opening the next sentence, and the sentence is now two sentences. **AND THE THIRD IS A ROW THE WHOLE LIST MISSED, WHICH IS THAT THREE OF THE TEN CARDS NAMED ONE BOLDED VOICE AND THE FIRST DRAFT OF 0692, 0694 AND 0697 GAVE THAT VOICE ONE TURN INSTEAD OF TWO, and no sentence row counts a turn. All three now carry two turns in two separate sections and the twenty attributions are twenty distinct five-word openings.**

**WHAT THIS BATCH DID NOT DO.** No chapter past 0700 was written. No new person, no descriptor, no name, no place by narration, no age or number given to anybody who had not got one. No notice written and no eighth asserted. No document arrived, read out, written in, shut or sent back. Nothing under the date on any of the ten. No second word in the column and no comparison of the two words. The second slate never picked up, turned over or written on, and no hand but the keeper's near its shelf. **The wall said nothing on all ten of these days and the panel count on these ten files is ZERO, and the words of the panel that stands on the page of Chapter 0690 are reprinted in no file this batch wrote, and no chapter of these ten says it out loud in a mouth or out of one.** The man of about fifty-seven is asked nothing and told nothing and the rope is not lifted and not cut and not taken up. The man of about thirty-nine is not asked about the space and the name is not printed and the space is not measured. The moved mark is not traced and no mouth in these ten days says that anybody moved anything. The consent fracture is not mended and the fifth condition is not given and the two women of about twenty-nine are never in one room. The box is shut, the bag is down, the wage is unpriced and Break is shut. The man of about thirty-seven is in none of the ten. Nobody gets stronger. `outline/ending.md` unopened, the planned ending untouched, no new enemy, no plan file edited, no calendar edited, and no controller file touched.

**THE NEXT PHASE IS `workspace/volume-14/close/PROMPT.md`, THE CLOSE OF VOLUME 14, WHICH WRITES NO PROSE AND STARTS NO SIXTH BATCH. Days 691 to 700 are written, day 700 is the last chapter of this volume, and the one panel of this volume is on the page of Chapter 0690 and the volume's new question was asked on day 698 and is unanswered. `outline/ending.md` is the close's to read and no batch may read it.**

## VOLUME 14, BATCH 0005 — REVIEW REPAIR PASS ON CHAPTERS 0691 TO 0700 AND ON THE BLOCKS ABOVE. THIS BLOCK IS THE NEWEST AND IT WINS OVER EVERY BLOCK ABOVE IT. NOTHING ABOVE IS DELETED AND THE FIRST EDITIONS OF THE FOUR THINGS CORRECTED IN THE BLOCKS ABOVE ARE STILL STANDING BESIDE THEIR CORRECTIONS.

**THE STORY DID NOT MOVE AND THAT IS THE FIRST THING THIS BLOCK SAYS.** No day, no weekday, no Bare-Month ordinal, no anchor, no form, no notice, no object count that holds, no cast member, no descriptor, no decision of record, no bolded turn and no panel moved, and **the planned ending is untouched.** Three chapter files had sentences repaired in them, 0691, 0696 and 0700, and the other seven were not opened for prose at all. **The review is at `logs/batch-0005.review.log` and the full repair is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-14-batch-0005.md` §12.**

**THE ONE BLOCKING FAULT WAS A MISSING FILE, AND IT IS NOW ON DISK. `workspace/volume-14/close/PROMPT.md` DID NOT EXIST, and the block above this one, `state/continuity.md` and `state/open-threads.md` each ended by naming it as the next phase, and none of the three made it. A volume of fifty chapters and fifty days reached its end with nothing to end it with, and the dispatcher would have found no phase. IT EXISTS NOW AND IT IS THE ONLY PHASE FILE THIS PASS CREATED. Its one next phase is `workspace/volume-15/outline/PROMPT.md`, and it is an outline and not a batch, because that volume is named in `outline/series.md` with four published beats and has no outline file. `outline/ending.md` is the close's to read and no batch may read it.**

**THE THREE THINGS IN THE CHAPTERS THEMSELVES.** (1) **Chapter 0700 contradicted itself on the most heavily policed object in the volume.** Its thirty-seventh line put the second slate on the shelf behind the book and, in the same sentence, said it was not on the same shelf as anything else. The plan is the authority and `outline/volume-14.md` §2, §7.3 and §20.6 all put that slate on the shelf BEHIND THE REGISTER, so the line now says it stands on the shelf behind that book and is not on a shelf of its own, and the closing line's *on the shelf beside it* agrees with it. **Chapter 0691 asserted the opposite, that the slate was not on the same shelf as anything in that room, and is repaired to the plan's position.** **The prohibition on the second slate and the bare piece of door sharing a shelf is untouched, because those are the two it is about and widening it to the register would be widening a prohibition to make a finding fit.** (2) **Chapter 0696's second bolded turn had a real attribution ambiguity.** The quote says *in that house behind me*, which only works from the fifty-seven's own doorstep, and the paragraph above it ended on the man of about sixty at the top of that road, and the quote carried no attribution. A two-sentence paragraph now stands between them. **The full handle of the man of about fifty-seven is still used once in that file, because one full form per chapter per person is the rule and the short form was used instead.** (3) **Three places in Chapter 0691 carried one construction twice in a row** and were made into three different kinds of sentence. **THE REVIEW'S DENSITY FIGURES FOR 0692 ARE PUBLISHED AS WRONG IN THE RECORD AT ITS §12.6, because *his own two hands* stands four times in 0692 and not nine and *that room* stands zero times in 0692 and not twenty-one, and no chapter was repaired against a figure that is not in it.**

**THE TWO THINGS IN THIS FILE THAT WERE WRONG, CORRECTED IN PLACE WITH THEIR FIRST EDITIONS STANDING.** **The 0697 passages that read *because the trough had no water in it* are corrected, three of them, and the correction is published beside the sentence rather than over it: the page of 0697 puts the empty trough and the one pass in one paragraph and joins them in no word, and the volume's method is that narration may not make that join, and this file was making it in the record of a page that does not.** And **the sentence saying that Chapter 0700 ADDS the man of about thirty-four telling the carrier that the board was washed once and not twice "which the card does not have" is backwards in both halves and is corrected above**: the card's `turn` field names that speech, the page does not contain it, the board speech is 0697's, and the page does not say that *nobody in that row stopped him* of the man of about thirty-nine but that nobody in that row said one word to him.

**THE TWO FIGURES IN THE BATCH RECORD THAT COULD NOT BE RE-RUN AS PUBLISHED, BOTH WITHDRAWN IN THEIR OWN WORDS BESIDE THEIR FIRST EDITIONS.** The pre-repair column in that record's §1 said it could be produced again from a named commit and seven of its ten word counts and eight of its ten bolded counts are not on disk, and the corrected column is at that record's §12.1. **A FIGURE THAT WAS WRONG AND WAS SILENTLY FIXED IS THE WORST ORDER FOR THOSE TWO FACTS TO ARRIVE IN, and this repair published the wrong figure beside the right one instead.** And the present-tense check in that record's §3 was printed with a group `grep -E` does not support, so its green cells were an artifact of a broken pattern; the corrected form and its figures are at that record's §12.2, **and the corrected first pass is 0 and 2 and not 0 and 1, because the first edition named one of the two narration faults and repaired it and did not see the other.** **A THIRD INSTRUMENT IN THAT RECORD HAD THE SAME FAULT AND WAS FOUND BY THIS PASS AND NOT BY THE REVIEW: the command that certifies the absence of an independent review anchors its pattern at the start of a line, and the harness writes that line behind a terminal escape, so the zero it published was an anchor artifact and not evidence.**

## VOLUME 14 BATCH 0004 — A REVIEW OF THE REPAIR PASS ABOVE, ITS TWO FINDINGS THAT WERE REAL, AND THE TEN CLOSINGS REPUBLISHED AGAIN. THIS BLOCK IS APPENDED AND NO LINE ABOVE IT IS EDITED.

The full disposition of all eight is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-14-batch-0004.md` §13 and nothing in this block is a second account of it. **Two files were edited, `chapter-0686.md` and `chapter-0688.md`, both inside one paragraph of narration around a bolded turn, and no card, no day, no cast member and no plan file moved.** The pre-review reference is commit `2549c68` and every figure below is re-runnable with `git show 2549c68:chapters/volume-14/chapter-XXXX.md`.

**THE TEN CLOSINGS, REPUBLISHED AGAIN, BECAUSE THE TEN CLOSINGS PUBLISHED AT LINE 721 ABOVE ARE THE PRE-REPAIR TEN AND THIS IS THE BLOCK THAT CORRECTS THEM.** 0681 a man taking both hands off a strap and putting them behind his back and a strap going slack. 0682 a board put into another man's two hands and the giver going on up the row with his arm down where it was. 0683 a man walking out of a room and standing at his own trestle with both hands flat on the wood. 0684 a trough set in water and a bucket taken in both hands and a man walking. 0685 a heap flattened with a boot and the print wiped on the stone and the heap left standing. **0686 a bag set down on a stranger's trestle, a hand put on the trestle beside it and taken off again, and the man sitting down on the bottom step in the place the bag had been standing while another man turns sideways on the stone to get by him. 0687 a hand turning the near edge of a sheet over with one finger so a print goes under the paper. 0688 a stool picked up by its back and set down under a bench. 0689 two fingers put into a dip in a bench and taken out again. 0690 a board set down face down on the ground at the foot of a trestle and left there.** Ten people doing ten different things and ten constructions. **0682's and 0690's shared construction, both ending on a man going on up a row without a board, is repaired and 0682's was the one rewritten, because §9 item 32 fixes 0682's want and not its closing and §9 item 40, §10 and 0690's own card fix 0690's. The arguable pair a reader could press is 0683 and 0687, both a person taking a thing out of a room, and it is published here rather than hidden. None of the ten closes on a door with a date on it, on about four people going past a surface, on a hand going into a pocket, on a room emptying, or on a man who said eleven words and is not going to say a twelfth.**

**THE TWO FAULTS, IN ONE LINE EACH, AND THE ONE THING THAT WAS TRUE OF THE FIRST AND WAS NOT ACTED ON.** **Chapter 0688:** the repair above did not delete a bad sentence, it deleted the concrete reaction to the woman of about fifty-two's turn — four say out loud, the rest say nothing, nobody thanks her, the keeper does not look up — and wrote a general line about thanking her in its place, and moved the keeper's paragraph from in front of that reaction to behind it, which put a pronoun about sitting at the far end of the room immediately before a different woman was introduced. **The keeper's paragraph is back in front of the reaction, the general line is out, the reaction names its speaker by where she is sitting and not by a pronoun, and *nobody thanked her for standing up in a room and saying it* is now *nobody thanked her for saying it out loud in there*, because the lead of that file has her sitting at the far end of the room with both hands on her knees and she does not stand until the last paragraph of the file.** **Chapter 0686:** the rewrite above fixed the closing and took the man's deliberate release of the bag with it — hand off the top of it, hand flat on the trestle beside it, hand off that as well — and that release is the moment the chapter is about. **It is back, in one sentence, with the hand put on the top of the bag before it goes to the trestle, because the file has him lift the bag and carry it out and a man cannot take a hand off a bag he is not touching. `did not know what was in it` is still out and the furniture position is still out and the last beat is unchanged.**

# VOLUME 14 CLOSE — Chapters 0651 to 0700, days 651 to 700, the close of the volume, and the standing state at day 700

**This block is appended and no line above it is edited. The full record is `state/volume-14-close.md` and this block is a pointer and not a substitute. Volume 14 is COMPLETE at Chapter 0700. Day 700 is a Monday on the founding rule and by the arithmetic and the three hundred and eighty-fifth day of that month, and the three hundred and eighty-fifth carries nothing and no chapter of the fifty attached a meaning to it.**

**THE FIFTY DAYS, RE-DERIVED FROM DAY 1 BEING A TUESDAY AND FROM NOTHING ELSE, AND CHECKED AGAINST `outline/volume-14.md` §14.3 ON THREE THINGS AT ONCE: FIFTY ROWS, FIFTY DISTINCT DAYS, NO DAY TWICE, NO GAP, AND ZERO MISMATCHES on chapter-equals-day, on the weekday, and on the Bare-Month ordinal being day less 315. Day 651 is a Monday and BM 336; day 700 is a Monday and BM 385; 700 − 651 = 49 = 7 × 7, and that the two ends are the same weekday is arithmetic and not a symmetry anybody is allowed to use. FIFTY-SEVEN phrases of the form the Nth day of the Bare Month stand across the fifty files, in all fifty of them, and all fifty-seven resolve to their own chapter's day, and nothing above the three hundred and eighty-fifth and nothing below the three hundred and thirty-sixth.**

**THE PRESSURE COLUMN OF THE DAY MAP IS THE ROTATION'S ROW AND IT WAS COUNTED TWICE AND THE TWO AGREE CELL FOR CELL: physical nine, character fifteen, political nine, discovery six, decision six, cost three, recovery two, summing to FIFTY on fifty distinct chapters, and the list of chapters whose map tag differs from the rotation row that lists it is EMPTY. NEITHER COLUMN WAS EDITED BY THIS CLOSE AND NO CHAPTER'S PLACE IN EITHER WAS MOVED.**

**THE ADRIAN COLUMN, MEASURED ON THE FIFTY FILES AND ON NOTHING ELSE: the string stands in ELEVEN files and the eleven are exactly the eleven the column names, 0653, 0655, 0658, 0661, 0665, 0671, 0679, 0680, 0685, 0692 and 0697, and the other thirty-nine return zero on both readings. THE MAP'S COLUMN, THE PLAN'S WANTS TABLE AND THE FIFTY FILES AGREE AT ELEVEN OF ELEVEN, CHAPTER FOR CHAPTER, and this is the first volume in seven in which a close has found NO disagreement between an Adrian column and the files it was written for. He is Stage 2 on all fifty days, he performs no working, no threshold is opened, the other world is not named once across the fifty, nobody offers him a workway and he asks for none, and he is aged nowhere. FIVE OF THE ELEVEN WANTS ARE OBTAINED AND SIX ARE NOT, and neither set is a reward and neither is a lesson, and no reviewer may read the five as this volume getting worse or the six as this volume getting better.**

**WHAT THE FIFTY FOUND, IN ONE PARAGRAPH. A room decided, out loud, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door and about nine people in it, that a hand's width of bare board under that date is not to be written in, that the decision is not to be written down, and that nobody outside that room is to be told — day 675, with no vote, no cooling, and the cost named first in about eleven words in the decider's own mouth, and nobody thanked. A piece of chalk came out of an inside breast pocket on day 678 and went back the same afternoon unused, with the cost named first in about seven words. A cost named in the decider's own mouth on day 689 and a cost named in the mouth of the person it is paid to, twice, on day 691, and nothing in that room was done about either at any hour of that day. A page was said out loud in a room of about nine people on day 690 and NOBODY IN THAT ROOM AGREED WITH IT, and the man who has been right about a page in about four seconds five volumes running was asked and said nothing, and the cost of that silence is a space about two fingers wide at the foot of the column between the other two on his own board, which nobody in that room asked him about. A room on day 676 said in narration that the decision had gone out of the room with nobody. A room on day 694 found out it had run out of things to do to a page and got no notice to say so. One of about four people a day stopped at that door on day 695, read the date, and did not go up that stair, and nobody in this city knows which of the four it was and nobody asked. A question was asked on day 698 to one man and not to a room, in her own mouth, in daylight, and he did not answer it and not one person in that room heard it. And a room stood empty of everybody but her on day 699, and the chapter says in narration that nobody came up that stair and does not say why.**

**THE THING THIS VOLUME SPENT, AND IT IS THE STANDING STATE AT DAY 700: the piece of board under that date means NOTHING WHATEVER. Not that the place is kept and not that the place was never in anybody's mind, and there is nothing on that door, or on any other surface in this city, that says a room over that market ever decided anything about that wood, and there is no person in this city that anybody could be shown who learned anything in that room. THE SURFACE IS UNCHANGED ON THE LAST DAY AND ON EVERY DAY, AND NO CHAPTER OF THE FIFTY PUT ANYTHING UNDER THAT DATE, WIDENED OR NARROWED THAT BARE PIECE, OR SAID WHAT IT MEANS, AND A LATER VOLUME MAY NOT SPEND THE SAME INSTRUMENT AGAIN IN ANY OTHER PLACE EITHER.**

**THE THREE THINGS THAT ARE OPEN AND ARE RECORDED RATHER THAN RESOLVED. FIRST, THE QUESTION OF DAY 698 IS ASKED AND UNANSWERED, and a later volume may ask it again in a mouth, in a room, in daylight, with a date on the door, and may not answer it, and may not ask the man of about thirty-nine what the missing name on his own board is, may not ask the man of about thirty-four what he wrote in the column on the right of that page, may not ask the woman of about twenty-nine what the space under the date was for, and the three may not be joined. SECOND, A MARK PUT DOWN ON A PALM IS ON A PALM AND ON THE WOOD OF A TRESTLE, AND A CLEAN PIECE AT THE NEAR EDGE OF A BOARD LIES UNDER GRIT, and they are two marks and not the same instrument, nobody has been told where either came from, no chapter of the fifty printed a figure for either and no later volume may print one, and no cause was supplied for either. THIRD, THE MOVED MARK OF DAY 440 IS STILL NOBODY'S AND IS TWO HUNDRED AND SIXTY DAYS LONG AT DAY 700, FOR THE SIXTH VOLUME RUNNING, and the woman whose line moved is on no page of this volume and is never going to be asked about it.**

**AND WHAT THE FIFTY DO NOT SUPPORT, WHICH IS PUBLISHED RATHER THAN SMOOTHED. The volume's own claim has three parts: that a bare piece of board was the only instrument this city had for telling afterwards that a person had chosen to leave a place alone; that the instrument stops working the moment a room says out loud that the place is being left alone on purpose, and stops working without a mark; and that the person who knew what it was for is the person who is not asked. THE FIFTY SUPPORT THE FIRST TWO, on day 675 as a scene and on days 676 and 700 as narration. THEY DO NOT SUPPORT THE THIRD: twenty-three of the fifty files say in one form or another that she was not asked, and not one of the fifty comes within a sentence of a reason, and no mouth and no narration in the fifty joins the not-asking to the decision. A reader who wants the fifty to prove the third part will find that they do not, and nothing was reworded to make the shape better.**

**THE NEXT PHASE IS `workspace/volume-15/outline/PROMPT.md`, THE OUTLINE PHASE FOR THE VOLUME AFTER THIS ONE. IT IS NOT A BATCH, IT IS NOT A CONTINUATION, AND IT IS NOT DECIDED HERE.** The volume after this one is named in the volume list of `outline/series.md` and carries a published central pressure, a midpoint, a climax, a resolution and a question, and its outline file has not been written; the work that has not run is the work of writing its plan. `state/volume-14-close.md` §14 carries the nine things that phase inherits and it does not decide the next volume's title, because a close that decides the next volume's spine has planned a volume.

# VOLUME 15, DAYS 701 TO 750 — SET BY THE VOLUME 15 OUTLINE PHASE. THE PLAN IS `outline/volume-15.md` AND EVERY FIGURE BELOW IS POINTED AT AND NOT RESTATED, because a state file is a record of a plan and the plan is the plan.

**THE STORY FIRST, AND IT IS FOUR SENTENCES.** About four people a day walk past the outside of a door at the foot of a stair at the back of a room over a market, and that row is the only instrument this city has for finding out that anybody was ever in that room, and it works by nothing but feet. The volume behind this one spent a hand's width of bare board under a date by deciding out loud, in that same room, not to write on it, and the board now means nothing whatever, and there is nothing on that door or anywhere else in this city that says a room over a market ever decided anything about it. **This volume spends the row itself, by deciding out loud, in daylight, with a date on the door, that the people who walk on it are not to be asked what is said in the room — and the cost falls on about four people a day who are outside the room, are not thanked, and will never know. On the resolution day the woman who keeps the register comes round the front of that building, puts herself on that row, and tells nobody.** None of this is a power.

**WHAT A WRITER OF VOLUME 15 NEEDS AND WHERE IT IS, IN ONE PLACE, WITH NOTHING RESTATED.** The plan is `outline/volume-15.md`. The day map, the pressure column and the Adrian column are at its §14.3 and are part of the map and not a note on it. The eleven wants, the thing his hands are on and whether he got it are at its §4.1 and a card may only take them. **THE THING THE VOLUME SPENDS AND WHOSE IT IS IS AT ITS §6.5. The first decision, its day and its seven words are at §6.6. The wall's one day is at §6.7. The fact that this volume turns on a thing and not on a word is at §6.8, and the measurement behind it is at §19.4. The mid-volume decision is at §8. The climax is at §10, the resolution at §11, the finding at §12, and the new question at §13. The calendar is `bible/power-system.md` §62, a new numbered section, and it amends nothing. **Day 708. Tuesday. The three hundred and ninety-third day of the Bare Month. Pressure: character. Adrian: no. Frame: §20.7 frame two.**

Whose speech is bolded: the man of about thirty-nine who trades on a board, once, and the man who keeps the stall two stalls along, once. **Third party or task: the man who keeps the stall two stalls along comes to the near end of that board and stands there, and the two of them have been at the same end of that market for nine years and neither has ever asked the other anything, and the third party here is a look and not a question.**

Sets this chapter names: the nineteen rates and the missing name. **Nineteen rates in three columns, and the count holds at nineteen and no rate is added and none is taken out. The foot of the middle column of the three is a bare space about two fingers wide and the name is not on that board and is not printed in this volume and this chapter does not ask him about it and does not put it in any mouth, and the chapter does not say he was right about anything.** **The word *row* in this chapter means the market row and the chapter says so in the sentence.**

**Closing movement: a discovery and a person leaving — his own right hand flat over that bare space and staying there, and the man two stalls along gone with his own two hands in his own sleeves, and neither of them said a word.**

Prohibitions in force: §16.5, §16.12, §16.13, §16.15, §16.16, §17.1, §17.5, §17.6, §17.7, §17.10, §17.12, §17.16, §17.17, §17.19, §17.20.

## Card 0709

**Day 709. Wednesday. The three hundred and ninety-fourth day of the Bare Month. Pressure: physical. Adrian: YES. Frame: none of the eight, and this card takes none.**

Whose speech is bolded: the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, once, and nobody else speaks. **Third party or task: the man of about twenty-seven who washes that board, who was on that row before Adrian was, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, whose own front in that market row a trough and a bucket standing out in the walking line put in the way of a person going to his own front.**

Sets this chapter names: the trough, the bucket and the rag; eleven miles of flats. **The rag is on the stone lip of the step and is not inside the trough. Four hundred yards of flags at one end of that market is a walk and this card does not turn it into a figure about a place. This chapter names no door, no date in chalk, no bare piece of door and no row at the foot of any stair, because the plan's own per-chapter column does not list them for this day.** **He wanted those flags to be dry by the fourth hour, and they are, and the man who washes that board was on that row before he was and did not use them — OBTAINED, and this card supplies none of that and may only take it.**

**Closing movement: a reversal and a person leaving — the flags were dry at the fourth hour and at the tenth, and about four people a day walk that market and not one of them counted anything, and the man who washes that board went down that row with his own empty bucket and did not use them.**

Prohibitions in force: §16.12, §16.13, §16.15, §17.1, §17.5, §17.6, §17.7, §17.10, §17.12, §17.16, §17.17, §17.18, §17.19, §17.20.

## Card 0710

**Day 710. Thursday. The three hundred and ninety-fifth day of the Bare Month. Pressure: recovery. Adrian: no. Frame: §20.7 frame four.**

Whose speech is bolded: the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, once, in that room. Nobody thanks him. **Third party or task: a chair, drawn out from under that shelf and put back, and the thing that has to be carried somewhere is nothing; the third party is about nine people in that room who all learn there is a shelf behind that bench and not one of whom is told what is on it.**

Sets this chapter names: the register and the two slates. **The register stands on that shelf and the second slate stands behind it on the same shelf, and nothing on that shelf was touched and no hand but the keeper's went near it at any hour of this day. The middle column holds five figures and no sixth. The column on the right carries one word near the head of one page and this chapter does not print it and does not put a word in it. The slate is not picked up, not turned over and not written on. The bare piece of door under the date is not in this chapter.**

**Closing movement: a cost named out loud and paid and not thanked, and the room the room it was at the fourth hour, and the only thing that happened in it being that a man said out loud that he did not know what was behind that shelf. THIS IS ONE OF THE TWO CLOSINGS IN THIS TEN THAT MAY BE A STATEMENT THAT NOTHING VISIBLE CHANGED, and the second one is in 0703, and no third exists.**

Prohibitions in force: §16.1, §16.3, §16.5, §16.12, §16.13, §16.15, §16.16, §17.1, §17.5, §17.6, §17.7, §17.10, §17.12, §17.16, §17.17, §17.19, §17.20.
